Total survey error framework \citep{Groves_2010}

Insight:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Errors can come from bias or variance
\item
  Total survey error = Measurement error + representation error
\end{enumerate}

\includegraphics{images/frame_0_delay-0.01s.jpg}

\citep[Fig. 3]{Groves_2010}

Probability and Non-probability Sampling

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Probability sample: every unit from a frame population has a known and non-zero probability of inclusion
\item
  With weighting, we can recover bias in your sampling.
\end{itemize}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Non-response problem
\end{itemize}

Horvitz-Thompson estimator (or bias estimator):

\[
\hat{\bar{y}} = \frac{\sum_{i \in s}y_i / \pi_i}{N}
\]

where \(\pi_i\) = person i's probability of inclusion (we have to estimate)

\href{https://www.allourideas.org/}{Wiki Survey}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Create a survey that leverages the power of people
\end{itemize}

Mass Collaboration

\begin{itemize}
\item
  Human Computation: Train People -\textgreater{} Train Lots of People -\textgreater{} Train Machine

  \begin{itemize}
  \item
    Cleaning
  \item
    De-biasing
  \item
    Combining
  \end{itemize}
\item
  Open Call:

  \begin{itemize}
  \item
    solutions are easier to check than generate
  \item
    required specialized skills
  \end{itemize}
\item
  Distributed Data Collection:

  \begin{itemize}
  \item
    People go out and collect data
  \item
    quality check
  \end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

\href{https://www.fragilefamilieschallenge.org/}{Fragile family challenges}

\hypertarget{experiment}{%
\chapter{Experiment}\label{experiment}}

Things to consider:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  Validity

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    statistical conclusion validity
  \item
    internal validity
  \item
    construct validity: whether you operationalize your construct correctly
  \item
    external validity: generalization
  \end{itemize}
\item
  Heterogeneity of treatment effects

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    average (aggregate) effect can be artificially constructed.
  \end{itemize}
\item
  Mechanisms
\end{itemize}

More things to consider

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  cost
\item
  control
\item
  realism
\item
  ethics
\end{itemize}

The Principles of Humane Experimental Technique by Russell and Burch (1959): 3Rs

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Replace: with natural (less invasive)
\item
  Refine: less harmful
\item
  Reduce: min number of participants
\end{itemize}

Before running experiments, to make sure that you did not tamper with the data, or hypotheses (i.e., change them after the fact), you should always pre-register your research. A website that allows you to do this and automatically provides you with time stamp is \href{https://aspredicted.org/}{aspredicted}

This way then you can show to reviewers that you did hypothesize before running analysis.

To combat replication crisis in psychology, we can advocate for open research in which researchers public their data and analyses. Popular websites include:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \href{https://researchbox.org/index.php}{ResearchBox}
\item
  \url{https://osf.io/}
\end{itemize}

To see whether authors temper with their data, we can use \href{http://p-curve.com/}{p-curve} to inspect. \href{https://datacolada.org/66}{1} \href{https://datacolada.org/67}{2} \href{https://datacolada.org/49}{3} \href{https://datacolada.org/45}{4} \href{https://datacolada.org/91}{5}

\href{https://datacolada.org/80}{Interaction Effects Need Interaction Controls} (basically, you can't just control for main effects).

Total survey error framework \citep{Groves_2010}

Insight:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Errors can come from bias or variance
\item
  Total survey error = Measurement error + representation error
\end{enumerate}

\citep[Fig. 3]{Groves_2010}

Probability and Non-probability Sampling

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Probability sample: every unit from a frame population has a known and non-zero probability of inclusion
\item
  With weighting, we can recover bias in your sampling.
\end{itemize}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Non-response problem
\end{itemize}

Horvitz-Thompson estimator (or bias estimator):

\[
\hat{\bar{y}} = \frac{\sum_{i \in s}y_i / \pi_i}{N}
\]

where \(\pi_i\) = person i's probability of inclusion (we have to estimate)

\href{https://www.allourideas.org/}{Wiki Survey}

\hypertarget{experiment-1}{%
\chapter{Experiment}\label{experiment-1}}

Things to consider:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  Validity

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    statistical conclusion validity
  \item
    internal validity
  \item
    construct validity: whether you operationalize your construct correctly
  \item
    external validity: generalization
  \end{itemize}
\item
  Heterogeneity of treatment effects

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    average (aggregate) effect can be artificially constructed.
  \end{itemize}
\item
  Mechanisms
\end{itemize}

More things to consider

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  cost
\item
  control
\item
  realism
\item
  ethics
\end{itemize}

The Principles of Humane Experimental Technique by Russell and Burch (1959): 3Rs

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Replace: with natural (less invasive)
\item
  Refine: less harmful
\item
  Reduce: min number of participants
\end{itemize}

Before running experiments, to make sure that you did not tamper with the data, or hypotheses (i.e., change them after the fact), you should always pre-register your research. A website that allows you to do this and automatically provides you with time stamp is \href{https://aspredicted.org/}{aspredicted}

This way then you can show to reviewers that you did hypothesize before running analysis.

To combat replication crisis in psychology, we can advocate for open research in which researchers public their data and analyses. Popular websites include:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \href{https://researchbox.org/index.php}{ResearchBox}
\item
  \url{https://osf.io/}
\end{itemize}

To see whether authors temper with their data, we can use \href{http://p-curve.com/}{p-curve} to inspect. \href{https://datacolada.org/66}{1} \href{https://datacolada.org/67}{2} \href{https://datacolada.org/49}{3} \href{https://datacolada.org/45}{4} \href{https://datacolada.org/91}{5}

\href{https://datacolada.org/80}{Interaction Effects Need Interaction Controls} (basically, you can't just control for main effects).

\hypertarget{formative-vs.-reflective-measurements}{%
\chapter{Formative vs.~Reflective Measurements}\label{formative-vs.-reflective-measurements}}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule
\begin{minipage}[b]{(\columnwidth - 2\tabcolsep) * \real{0.04}}\raggedright
\strut
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{(\columnwidth - 2\tabcolsep) * \real{0.43}}\raggedright
Reflective\strut
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{(\columnwidth - 2\tabcolsep) * \real{0.52}}\raggedright
Formative\strut
\end{minipage}\tabularnewline
\midrule
\endhead
\begin{minipage}[t]{(\columnwidth - 2\tabcolsep) * \real{0.04}}\raggedright
Root\strut
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[t]{(\columnwidth - 2\tabcolsep) * \real{0.43}}\raggedright
Common factor model\strut
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[t]{(\columnwidth - 2\tabcolsep) * \real{0.52}}\raggedright
Principal Component Analysis (Weighted Linear Composites)\strut
\end{minipage}\tabularnewline
\begin{minipage}[t]{(\columnwidth - 2\tabcolsep) * \real{0.04}}\raggedright
\strut
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[t]{(\columnwidth - 2\tabcolsep) * \real{0.43}}\raggedright
Construct as causes of measures\strut
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[t]{(\columnwidth - 2\tabcolsep) * \real{0.52}}\raggedright
Measures as causes of construct\strut
\end{minipage}\tabularnewline
\begin{minipage}[t]{(\columnwidth - 2\tabcolsep) * \real{0.04}}\raggedright
Dimensionality\strut
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[t]{(\columnwidth - 2\tabcolsep) * \real{0.43}}\raggedright
Interchangeable

Measures = reflection of construct in its entirety (Unidimensionality of reflective measures)

Useful redundancy\strut
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[t]{(\columnwidth - 2\tabcolsep) * \real{0.52}}\raggedright
Multidimensionality of formative measures (this is a liability because it is analogous to multibarralled items) because the variance of the latent variable is determined by the paths (i.e., strength) from the measures, and variances and covariances of the measures.\strut
\end{minipage}\tabularnewline
\begin{minipage}[t]{(\columnwidth - 2\tabcolsep) * \real{0.04}}\raggedright
Internal Consistency\strut
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[t]{(\columnwidth - 2\tabcolsep) * \real{0.43}}\raggedright
Reflective measures should correlate positively\strut
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[t]{(\columnwidth - 2\tabcolsep) * \real{0.52}}\raggedright
No needs for the formative measures to correlate

Correlation among measures can be problem (multicollinearity).

Higher the correlation (among measures), more unstable the loadings on construct, larger standard errors.

The lack of internal consistency does not provide evidence for a set of measures to be formative.\strut
\end{minipage}\tabularnewline
\begin{minipage}[t]{(\columnwidth - 2\tabcolsep) * \real{0.04}}\raggedright
Identification\strut
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[t]{(\columnwidth - 2\tabcolsep) * \real{0.43}}\raggedright
identified only when either

\begin{itemize}
\item
  at least 3 measures, and fix scale variance to 1 or fix a loading to 1.
\item
  2 measures, and loadings, are set equal to each other, or latent variable covary with anther latent variables that has at least 2 reflective measures.
\end{itemize}\strut
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[t]{(\columnwidth - 2\tabcolsep) * \real{0.52}}\raggedright
are not identified, unless 2 additional reflective measures are added to be caused by the construct- multiple indicator multiple cause (MIMIC) model.

Hence, the choice of reflective measures for identification concern can change the loadings relating the measures to its latent variable, which in turn, change the construct itself. Hence, specifying the reflective measures in MIMIC model is monumental\strut
\end{minipage}\tabularnewline
\begin{minipage}[t]{(\columnwidth - 2\tabcolsep) * \real{0.04}}\raggedright
Measurement Error\strut
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[t]{(\columnwidth - 2\tabcolsep) * \real{0.43}}\raggedright
independent measurement errors\strut
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[t]{(\columnwidth - 2\tabcolsep) * \real{0.52}}\raggedright
No measurement error (i.e., error-free indicators)\strut
\end{minipage}\tabularnewline
\begin{minipage}[t]{(\columnwidth - 2\tabcolsep) * \real{0.04}}\raggedright
Construct Validity\strut
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[t]{(\columnwidth - 2\tabcolsep) * \real{0.43}}\raggedright
construct validity is manifested by the the magnitudes of the loadings relating the measures to the construct, which determined by the covariances among measures (e.g., the higher covariances the stronger correspondence)\strut
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[t]{(\columnwidth - 2\tabcolsep) * \real{0.52}}\raggedright
\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  strength between construct and its measures
\item
  proportion of variance in the construct attributed to the residual \(\zeta\) (smaller better)
\item
  In relation to other variables (nomological validity and criterion-oriented validity)
\item
  Indirect effect of formative measure on reflective measure via the construct.
\end{enumerate}

Drawbacks:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  estimates are sensitive to the chose of reflective measures as mentioned in the identification section.
\item
  Nomological network could not be empirically validated because of the conceptual heterogeneity in the formative measures.
\end{itemize}\strut
\end{minipage}\tabularnewline
\begin{minipage}[t]{(\columnwidth - 2\tabcolsep) * \real{0.04}}\raggedright
Causality\strut
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[t]{(\columnwidth - 2\tabcolsep) * \real{0.43}}\raggedright
construct to measures

construct = latent variable

From a realist perspective, construct = real entity (i.e., could influence) (e.g., psychological state), measures = scores\strut
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[t]{(\columnwidth - 2\tabcolsep) * \real{0.52}}\raggedright
measures to construct

construct = composite variable

From an operationalist or instrumentalist perspective, formative measures define the construct (i.e., composite score)

I'd suggest to think of formative constructs as processes

Under constructivist perspective, constructs can be ascribed theoretical meaning, but would only be a conceptual entity

But the causal thinking is inappropriate (because measures and construct are not 2 distinct entities), they are part-whole correspondence\strut
\end{minipage}\tabularnewline
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}

According to \citep{edwards2010},

\[
x_i = \lambda_i \xi + \delta_i
\]

where

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \(x_i\) = reflective measure
\item
  \(\xi\) = construct
\item
  \(\lambda_i\) = effect of \(\xi\) on \(x_i\)
\item
  \(\delta_i\) = uniqueness of the measure (error)
\end{itemize}

\[
\eta = \gamma_i x_i + \zeta
\]

where

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \(\eta\) = construct
\item
  \(\gamma_i\) = effect of \(x_i\) on \(\eta\)
\item
  \(\zeta\) = residual (unexplained part of \(\eta\))
\end{itemize}

In case that \(\eta\) is a perfect weighted linear combination of \(x_i\), then

\[
\eta = \gamma_i x_i
\]

\hypertarget{alternative-to-formative-model}{%
\section{Alternative to formative model}\label{alternative-to-formative-model}}

\begin{itemize}
\item
  Model with first-order constructs instead of measures, and each with single reflective measures

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    can't estimate the loadings and unique variances of the reflective measures
  \end{itemize}
\item
  Model with first-order constructs instead of measure, but each with three reflective measures, and second-order construct also has two reflective measures.

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    sometimes, first-order construct can't be measured multiple times (e.g., income, age)
  \end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[t]{(\columnwidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.09}}\raggedright
500\strut
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[t]{(\columnwidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.30}}\raggedright
summarize findings\strut
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[t]{(\columnwidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.29}}\raggedright
Present (implications and future search)

Past (completed research)\strut
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[t]{(\columnwidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.20}}\raggedright
\strut
\end{minipage}\tabularnewline
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}

Rules of thumb:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  Past: events that have been completed

  \begin{itemize}
  \item
    We found
  \item
    Method proposed by X was followed
  \end{itemize}
\item
  Present Perfect: events that started in the past but are still ongoing

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    Many studied have researched on
  \end{itemize}
\item
  Present: events that are general fact, and current
\end{itemize}

Journal paper types:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Research Paper: hypotheses and findings
\item
  Methods Paper: new method
\item
  Review Paper: consolidate a domain
\item
  Discussion Paper: opinions regarding other papers
\end{enumerate}

\hypertarget{review-process}{%
\chapter{Review Process}\label{review-process}}

Communicate your review by O. C. Ferrell:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  Paragraph

  \begin{enumerate}
  \def\labelenumii{\arabic{enumii}.}
  \item
    contextualize the research
  \item
    convey the key message of the research
  \item
    be positive about what was done well
  \end{enumerate}
\item
  Paragraph:

  \begin{enumerate}
  \def\labelenumii{\arabic{enumii}.}
  \item
    Is the paper well written, interesting and important?
  \item
    Are the methods, data, etc. appropriate?
  \item
    Does the data support the findings?
  \end{enumerate}
\item
  Paragraphs: Numbers, topics-examples of an issue:

  \begin{enumerate}
  \def\labelenumii{\arabic{enumii}.}
  \item
    Factual errors
  \item
    Invalid arguments
  \item
    length/journal guidance
  \item
    Paths to improve the research
  \end{enumerate}
\end{enumerate}

Documenting your reviews

\begin{itemize}
\item
  Use publons.com to document manuscripts you reviewed for promotion and CV
\item
  to verify your review, send the ``thank you for reviewing'' email to reviews@publons.com
\end{itemize}

The review process:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  Journals always look for reviewers

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    Get involved and email your CV to editor, or area editor.
  \end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Do not wait to answer whether you review or not.
\end{itemize}

Responding to reviews

\begin{itemize}
\item
  Turn around in 3 months
\item
  R \& R: reject and resubmit (new). The turn around will be sent to a new set of reviewers
\end{itemize}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  R \& R: review and resubmit.
\end{itemize}

Types fo Consumer Research:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  Theory Development
\item
  Theory Application

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    limited impact on managerial practice
  \end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

Theory-Testing vs.~Substantive Phenomena-Driven Consumer Research

\citep{janiszewski2016}

\begin{itemize}
\item
  Deductive-Conceptual Knowledge

  \begin{itemize}
  \item
    A theory is ``a statement of concepts and their interrelationships that shows how and/or why a phenomenon occurs.'' \citep{corley2011}
  \item
    Knowledge Creation Strategies:

    \begin{itemize}
    \item
      Bridging disciplines
    \item
      Challenging assumptions
    \item
      Introducing mediators and moderators
    \item
      Contrastive explanation
    \item
      Borrowing and blending
    \end{itemize}
  \item
    Knowledge Appreciation

    \begin{itemize}
    \item
      Quality of the contribution

      \begin{itemize}
      \tightlist
      \item
        depends on rigor, and execution of research method
      \end{itemize}
    \item
      Benefit of the contribution

      \begin{itemize}
      \item
        Originality / Novelty
      \item
        Interestingness
      \item
        Changes in core understanding/beliefs
      \end{itemize}
    \end{itemize}
  \end{itemize}
\item
  Integration (i.e., tree of knowledge)
\end{itemize}

\hypertarget{social-influence}{%
\section{Social Influence}\label{social-influence}}

\citep{mcferran2010}

\begin{itemize}
\item
  body type of others influences one's food consumption

  \begin{itemize}
  \item
    moderated by body type (undesirable reference group) of the other consumers
  \item
    low appearance self-esteem or cognitive load also moderate this relationship
  \end{itemize}
\item
  Anchoring and adjustment processes
\item
  Social Influence and Food Choice

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    people conform to group average (anchor)
  \end{itemize}
\item
  Study 1: Social influence effects is present regardless of food perception (healthy vs.~unhealthy). People eat more when there is another person, but the magnitude is moderated by the other's body type (consume less when seeing obese others - undesirable group). The effect persists after social influence.
\item
  Study 2: Presence of other increases food quantity.

  \begin{itemize}
  \item
    High anchor (obese person takes high quantity), participants take less.
  \item
    Low anchor (obese person takes small quantity), participants take more
  \end{itemize}
\item
  Study 3: People with low appearance self-esteem and high processing resources will attenuate more
\end{itemize}

\citep{moreau2010}

\begin{itemize}
\item
  Social comparison: people want to gain self-knowledge.

  \begin{itemize}
  \item
    Self-designed products are based on:

    \begin{itemize}
    \item
      comparison with the characteristics of other comparable products
    \item
      comparison with skills, talents, and expertise of the other designers.
    \end{itemize}
  \end{itemize}
\item
  Since professional designers are expert, consumer designers face upward comparison
\item
  Study 1:

  \begin{itemize}
  \item
    a. Self-deigned product evaluation is lower when compared to professional default product, than consumer designed default design
  \item
    b. Firm guidance moderates this effect
  \item
    There is a premium for self-designed product.
  \end{itemize}
\item
  Study 2: Defensive vs.~Nondefensive processing

  \begin{itemize}
  \item
    need to protect self-esteem/ self-image.
  \item
    a. At low defensive processing, evaluations of self-designed products are lower when the default product is professionally designed than consumer designed
  \item
    defensive processing reduces negative comparison
  \end{itemize}
\item
  Study 3: The behavioral consequences of social comparison in self-design.

  \begin{itemize}
  \item
    Public prize (visibility) enhance self-evaluations.
  \item
    a. Repair opportunity affects the evaluation of self-designed products
  \end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

\citep{hamilton2003}

\begin{itemize}
\item
  Influence based on context effects (suggesting other unlikely alternative)

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    moderated by the belief whether others are trying to manipulate the choice context.
  \end{itemize}
\item
  People understand context effects (e.g., choice set construction).
\item
  To influence others, people construct a set of unattractive alternatives (attraction effect), or a compromise between two other alternatives (compromise effect).
\item
  Author hypothesized: People correct the contextual effects when they know constructors' intentions (to influence them).
\item
  Results: ``Subjects' beliefs that menus had been created \textbf{(by their friends)} to influence their choices seemed to enhance rather than limit the effectiveness of the menus.'' (p.~498) likely due to homophily bias.

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    Evaluated the target more favorably.
  \end{itemize}
\item
  Source credibility, expertise, trustworthiness, attractiveness, and similarity can influence the effectiveness of persuasion attempt \citep{wilson1993}
\item
  Hypothesis: ulterior motive of persuader moderates increase the resistant effect of chooser than group-oriented motive.

  \begin{itemize}
  \item
    Subjects are likely to agree (choose) when they know the menu is created by their friends
  \item
    knowing that the menu has been created by a stranger, under compromise strategy, subjects still choose the middle alternative.
  \end{itemize}
\item
  Attraction strategy is more likely to be used than compromise strategy
\item
  Even if subjects understand contextual effects (manipulation), they do not resist because

  \begin{itemize}
  \item
    it's hard to recognize characteristics of a local set from the characteristics of a global set. \citep{simonson1993}
  \item
    If subjects assume that the most attractive option has been eliminated, they have to choose the best among alternatives.
  \end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

\citep{argo2005}

\begin{itemize}
\item
  Non interactive social presence in the consumption context.
\item
  social forces has greatest influence \citep{latan?1981}:

  \begin{itemize}
  \item
    number (social size) (large \textgreater{} small):

    \begin{itemize}
    \tightlist
    \item
      emotions and behaviors: (e.g., stage fright, crowding)
    \end{itemize}
  \item
    immediacy (proximity) (closer \textgreater{} far)
  \item
    social source strength (importance) (high \textgreater{} low)
  \end{itemize}
\item
  Social Impact Theory
\item
  Study 1:

  \begin{itemize}
  \item
    0 -\textgreater{} 1 person increased positive emotions
  \item
    1 -\textgreater{} 3 person decreased positive emotions
  \end{itemize}
\item
  Study 2:

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    proximity moderates the impact of social size on emotions and brand selection.
  \end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

\hypertarget{interpersonal-perception-and-consumer-lay-beliefs}{%
\section{Interpersonal perception and consumer lay beliefs}\label{interpersonal-perception-and-consumer-lay-beliefs}}

\citep{folkes2003}

\begin{itemize}
\item
  Info valence: \textbf{Negativity bias for products} (negative info about a product has greater influence on brand perceptions than positive info). \citep{herr1991}
\item
  \textbf{Positivity bias for services} (positive experience from a service provider can lead customers to infer positivity about the brand as a whole than negative experience).

  \begin{itemize}
  \item
    Could be because service is more heterogeneous.
  \item
    this effect is greater for novice consumer.
  \end{itemize}
\item
  Study 1: First time car insurance buyers

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    Alternative explanation: Subtyping \citep{weber1983}, or occupational stereotype consistency, question order are all ruled out.
  \end{itemize}
\item
  Study 2: replicate study 1: compare the effect of info about individual service provider to that of others who is not from the firm.

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    Subjects perceive focal firm employee more positively and consider negative experience from the other firms' agents as outliers.
  \end{itemize}
\item
  Because consumers are predisposed to think that a service encounter in general should be more positive than negative \citep{fornell2005}, this assumption could explain why consumers consider positive experience as typical and generalize it to the brand, and negative experience as outliers.
\item
  3 sources that affect consumers' beliefs:

  \begin{itemize}
  \item
    general perceptions of services
  \item
    beliefs specific to a firm
  \item
    beliefs specific to an occupation
  \end{itemize}
\item
  Study 3 used a critical incident method to examine the following hypothesis: the positive effect is moderated by consumer experience (e.g., stronger for less experience consumers).

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    Valence affects perceived typicality , but experience with a firm did not influence typicality (cant find support for H3)
  \end{itemize}
\item
  Study 4: Replicate study 1 and 2 in a natural setting
\end{itemize}

\citep{brough2016}

\begin{itemize}
\item
  Men are less likely to buy environmentally friendly products than women because the close association between green behavior and femininity (perceived by both men and women, and both users and observers).

  \begin{itemize}
  \item
    Prior research attribute this gap to personality traits (women are more prosocial altruistic \citep{lee1999}, and stronger ethic of care \citep{zelezny2000})
  \item
    The link between greenness and femininity could be explained by

    \begin{itemize}
    \item
      target of green marketing usually involved women (e.g., cleaning, laundry).
    \item
      caring and nurturing from greenness are feminine traits
    \end{itemize}
  \end{itemize}
\item
  Since men are more concerned with gender -identity maintenance (which is kinda surprising from my view), they avoid purchasing green products which might jeopardize their macho image.

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    From social-identity theory: self-concept is also derived from perceived group membership \citep{turner1986}
  \end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

\citep{wang2016}

\begin{itemize}
\item
  Smile affects 2 social judgments: warmth and competence

  \begin{itemize}
  \item
    Broader smile leads to more warmth, but less competent

    \begin{itemize}
    \item
      Consumers focus moderates this effect

      \begin{itemize}
      \item
        Promotion-focused consumers (and low-risk consumption) like bigger smile on warmth
      \item
        Prevention-focused consumers (and high-risk consumption) like slight smile (signal competent)
      \end{itemize}
    \end{itemize}
  \end{itemize}
\item
  Based on stereotype content model (SCM) of social judgments \citep{fiske2002a}
\item
  Facial configuration has evolutionary proposes.
\end{itemize}

\citep{haws2017}

\begin{itemize}
\item
  Consumers believe healthier foods are more expensive than less healthy foods.
\item
  Due to the dual process model, intuitions based on biased information (processed heuristically) will lead consumers evaluating healthy claims with higher standard (e.g., higher standard for intuition-inconsistent than consistent claim).
\item
  Lay theories or lay beliefs about food = expensive intuition affects consumer decision making process, because this process is low involvement one.
\end{itemize}

\hypertarget{model-building}{%
\chapter{Model Building}\label{model-building}}

\[
U_A = V - P_A - t |x-a| \\
U_B = V - P_B - t|b-x|
\]

where

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \(\bar{x}\) = marginal consumer = indifferent consumer
\item
  \(-t|x-a|\) = Disutility of consuming a product away from ideal

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    transportation cost parameters, weights on the disuility incur when they purchase a product different from the ideal point.
  \end{itemize}
\item
  V = reservation price = max max that the customer is willing to take
\item
  x = location of marginal customer - customer who is indifference from buying from firm A and firm B.
\end{itemize}

To find the point that a consumer is indifference from buying from firm A and buying from firm B, we equate \(U_A = U_B\)

\[
V - P_A - t(\bar{x}-a) = V - P_B - t (b-\bar{x}) \\
P_B - P_A = 2t \bar{x}+ at - tb \\
\bar{x} = \frac{b-a}{2} + \frac{P_B - P_A}{2t}
\]

Market Share for firm A = \(\bar{x}\)

Market Share for firm B = \(1 - \bar{x}\)

\[
\pi_A = P_A (\frac{b-a}{2} + \frac{P_B - P_A}{2t}) \\
\pi_B = P_B (1 - \frac{b-a}{2} - \frac{P_B - P_A}{2t})
\]

\[
\frac{d\pi_A}{d P_A} = \frac{p \pi_B}{d P_B} = 0
\]

\[
\frac{P_B}{2t} - \frac{2 P_A}{2t} + \frac{b-a}{2} = 0
\]

and

\[
\frac{d^2\pi_A}{dP^2_A} = \frac{1}{t} < 0
\]

\[
1 - \frac{b-a}{2} - \frac{P_B}{t} + \frac{P_A}{2t} = 0 \\
1/2 + \frac{b-a}{4} - \frac{3 P_A}{4t} = 0 \\
\frac{t(2 + b- a)}{3}= P_A \\
\frac{1}{2} - \frac{b-a}{4} + \frac{2 + b - a}{12} = \frac{P_B}{2t} \\
\frac{4/3} - \frac{b-a}{3} = \frac{P_B}{t} \\
\frac{t(4 - b+a)}{3} = P_B
\]

\hypertarget{sales}{%
\chapter{Sales}\label{sales}}

\citep{habel2021} found that consistent with traditional idea, having variable factor in salesperson's compensation plan will increase performance, but also introduce health hazard (more stress which results in emotional exhaustion and more sick days). In cases where salespeople have higher personal ability and more social resources, this harmful effect is mitigated.
